CHARACTER: Wren Ashgrove, Half-Elf bard 2, acolyte background (as the sheet is now, after the last round; the level and HP may have been lower earlier)
== OPENING ==
Your character is complete.

Marrowford sits where the slow brown Marrow River bends under a stone bridge, a town of grain barges, tallow-chandlers and market stalls that never quite close. The lands around it are old and quarrelsome. Minor noble houses hold the river-farms, trade in favours, and pay well for stories that make their lineages look older than they are.

Wren Ashgrove is nineteen, half-elf, a bard with a quick grin and a sharper tongue. She was raised by Corvin Tallowmere, who taught her to spin tales and sell them. A few weeks ago she slipped away from his caravan at a river crossing in the night. She took his ledger of forged tales, its pages now hidden in the binding of her road-psalter, a few of his best forgeries, and a head start. She also carries a brass lantern-shaped reliquary of the Lantern-Bearer, the gentle road-god of travelers and tale-tellers, with a curl of old parchment inside that may be a true blessing or just another forgery.

Dusk settles over Marrowford's square. Lamps are being lit along the stalls, and the smell of river fish and woodsmoke hangs in the air. At the square's far edge, half in shadow, stands a wagon painted with a shrine-lantern sigil you know too well. Corvin's. There's no sign of the man himself.

A weathered driver lounges on the box, picking his teeth. Before him a young page in a minor noble's livery, pale and sweating, is pressing a sealed letter into his hand and glancing over his shoulder. The wax seal is one you've seen in the ledger. On the tailboard behind them sits a locked, iron-bound strongbox, begging to be poked at.

The crowd is thin enough that you could slip closer unseen, march up with a grin, or wait and watch. What does Wren do?

== ROUND 1 ==
PLAYER: I keep to the shadow of a grain-stall and slip closer, trying to stay unseen and get near enough to hear what the page says to the driver. While I'm at it, I pull out my road-psalter and flip to the margin notes to check whose seal that is in the ledger.

  [game] notebook: updated The Sealed Letter
  [game] time: 10 minutes later: Day 1, 6:40 PM
DM: Your Stealth check was a 14. You slide from stall to stall, a sack of barley and a hanging rack of tallow candles covering you, and end up crouched behind a water barrel six feet from the wagon's wheel.

The page's voice shakes. "It's for Master Tallowmere's hand only, understand? From Lord Edric Verrel of Thornhallow. The assessor arrives in three days to inspect the lineage charter, and my lord says the tale *must* be mended before then. He said Master Tallowmere would know what he meant."

The driver turns the letter over, unimpressed. "Master's gone upriver. Back in a day, maybe two. I'll see he gets it." He holds out a palm, and the page drops a coin into it. The boy hesitates, then hurries off toward the bridge, looking over his shoulder the whole way.

Meanwhile, you flipped open the psalter and thumbed the binding pages by the dimming light. Your Investigation check was an 8. You know you've seen a seal like that in the ledger, but the ink is cramped and the light is poor, and you couldn't place it before the page was done talking. With the ledger open and a lamp, you'd settle it.

The driver tucks the letter inside his coat, hops down, and wanders toward a tavern across the square, leaving the wagon unattended, with the strongbox on its tailboard. The page is still visible, crossing the bridge. Dusk is deepening.

What does Wren do?

== ROUND 2 ==
PLAYER: Mage Hand first, I send the spectral hand over to test the strongbox's latch and see if it's actually locked or just looks it, while I stay hidden behind the barrel. If the coast is clear, I'll slip over and look at the lock for a Sleight of Hand attempt, though I don't have thieves' tools, so I'll improvise with the dagger tip or a hairpin from my diplomat's pack.

  [DM lookup (hidden)] {"name": "Thieves' Tools"}
  [game] dice Wren's Dexterity check to pick the strongbox lock with a hairpin (no thieves' tools proficiency), DC 15: 1d20+2 = 1d20 [10] + 2 = 12
  [game] time: 3 minutes later: Day 1, 6:43 PM
DM: You cast Mage Hand, and a pale, shimmering hand drifts out from behind the barrel, low along the cobbles. It tugs at the strongbox's latch. The latch rattles but holds, so the box really is locked. You dismiss the hand before it can draw an eye.

The driver has vanished into the tavern, and the square's traffic is thinning. You slip over, kneel at the tailboard, and go to work on the lock with a hairpin. It's a good lock, heavy and well oiled, with a tight keyway. Your check was a 12 against a DC 15. The pin slips, bends, and snaps with a thin *tink*, leaving a sliver of metal wedged in the keyway.

A fishwife three stalls down glances up at the sound, then goes back to counting her coin. Nobody else notices, but the lock is now harder to pick. You'd need a better tool than a hairpin, and the damage will be obvious to anyone who looks.

Meanwhile the page is still crossing the bridge, and the driver will return to his wagon sooner or later. The wagon is Corvin's, so there's a chance he left more than a box here.

What does Wren do?

== ROUND 3 ==
PLAYER: I give up on the box and quietly check the wagon itself, slipping around to the back and peeking under the canvas flap for anything else Corvin left, like a satchel, a second ledger, or a shrine altar. If I find nothing in a minute or two, I'll tail the page across the bridge and ask him a few friendly questions about Lord Verrel's "mended tale."

  [game] notebook: updated Corvin's Wagon

  [DM lookup (hidden)] {"name": "Forgery Kit"}
DM: Your Investigation check was a 19. You lift the canvas flap and slip inside the cramped wagon. Under the smell of lamp oil and old wool, your trained eye finds what a casual one wouldn't.

A small folding travelling shrine of the Lantern-Bearer stands against the front panel. Its back panel sits a hair proud of the frame, and behind it is a flat leather case. Inside are sheets of blank vellum, a tin of wax-impressions, a Verrel seal among them, and a half-finished draft in Corvin's neat, unmistakable hand. It reads: *"...to be mended before the assessor arrives. The Verrel charter's third descent must hold, or the ink on the whole line will not."* Beneath it is a list of three noble houses with sums beside them, two struck through and one circled: Verrel.

So Corvin is fixing his own forgery, and he knows it's about to be exposed. There's no second ledger and no satchel, only a cot, a cloak, and the case. You could take the draft, copy it, or leave it as you found it. The sealing-wax impressions would be useful to a forger.

Outside, the tavern door bangs. The driver hasn't come back yet, but a minute or two is all you have. The page is still on the far side of the bridge, getting smaller.

What does Wren do with the case, and then do you still tail the page?

== ROUND 4 ==
PLAYER: I snatch the wax-impression tin and quickly copy the draft's key lines onto a sheet of my own paper with my pen, then slip the original back behind the shrine panel exactly as I found it so Corvin never knows. Then I slide out the back of the wagon, keep to the shadows, and tail the page across the bridge.

DM: You work fast. The tin goes into your pouch, and your pen scratches the key lines of the draft onto a sheet of your own paper. You tuck the original back behind the shrine panel exactly as it was, then ease out the rear flap and into the lengthening shadows.

The tail goes badly. The page is on the middle of the bridge when a barge horn blasts below, and your shoe scuffs a loose cobble. Your Stealth check was a 9, just under his alertness, and the boy spins around. For a heartbeat you're two figures facing each other across twenty feet of lamplit stone, with the water sliding by underneath.

He's about fourteen, freckled and ink-stained at the fingers, and he looks ready to bolt. One hand is on a belt-knife far too big for him. "Who's there? Did Master Hale send you?" he says, voice cracking. "I delivered it! I swear I gave it to the driver!"

Whoever Master Hale is, the boy clearly fears him, and the boy thinks you might be him or work for him. He hasn't run yet.

What does Wren say or do?

== ROUND 5 ==
PLAYER: I step into the lamplight with my hands open and a warm, lopsided grin, keeping my voice low and friendly. "Easy, easy, I'm nobody's Master Hale. I'm just a storyteller who saw a brave lad deliver a letter under a lot of pressure. Put the knife down, and tell me, who is Master Hale, and what's he got on you that has you so scared?"

DM: You step into the lamplight, hands open, with your best lopsided grin. It doesn't land. Your Persuasion check was a 7.

Tobin's eyes flick over you: the rapier at your hip, the road-worn cloak, the smile. His knuckles whiten on the knife hilt. "That's what they all do," he says, voice tight. "Hale's men smile. Hale's men always smile." He takes a step back along the bridge rail. "I told you, I gave it to the driver. That's all I was told to do. Leave me alone or I'll shout for the bridge-warden."

Behind him, at the far end of the bridge, a bored-looking warden with a lantern pole leans in a doorway, thirty paces off. One yell and he'll be here.

You've learned that Master Hale exists, that Tobin fears him, and that Hale sends smiling people after Tobin. The boy is a hair from bolting, and he clearly won't be talked round by charm alone.

What does Wren do?

DM: You unbuckle the rapier and lay it on the stone rail between you, then flick a silver piece into the lamplight. Your Persuasion check was a 23.

Tobin stares at the blade, the coin and your face. Whatever he's looking for in it, the smile isn't there, and that seems to be what he needed. The knife drops an inch, then two. "You're... *running* from him?" His voice goes small. "Master Tallowmere frightened me worse than anyone. He looked at me like he was reading me." He pockets the coin and steps back toward you, still wary. "Take your sword. I'm not a fool. I only don't want to be left alone with this."

Once you sit on the bridge rail, it comes out in a rush.

Master Hale is Lord Edric's steward-clerk, a cold, smiling man who keeps the household's money and has been "asking" Tobin what his lord sends to the storyteller, and to whom. Tobin thinks Hale means to sell the secret, either to the assessor's side or to a rival of Lord Edric's. The charter, he says, was bought from Corvin years ago, and Lord Edric has barely slept since the assessor's letter came. Tobin was told to hand the letter to the wagon driver and ride on to Thornhallow tonight to say it was done. He's lodging for the night at the Drowned Candle, an inn on the near bank, and is meant to leave at dawn.

He adds, quieter, "If Hale learns it was delivered, my lord is ruined. If he learns I talked to you, so am I."

The bridge-warden has lost interest in you. The lamps are lit and the river is dark below.

What does Wren do next?

PLAYER: I pick up the rapier, buckle it back on, and give Tobin a small, serious nod. "Then I'll keep your secret, and you keep mine. Tell me this, Tobin: if Hale wanted to get at Lord Edric's charter before the assessor does, where would he keep it, and would your lord trust a stranger who could mend that third descent before it's inspected?"

DM: You buckle the rapier back on. Tobin watches you do it, then chews his lip, thinking hard in the way of someone who has never been asked a question like this.

"The charter's in an iron chest in my lord's study, at Thornhallow, under the window. The key's on a chain around his neck. But Hale has a copy; I've seen him use it when my lord's asleep." He swallows. "As for trust... my lord doesn't trust strangers. He doesn't trust anyone but Master Tallowmere, and he's frightened because Master Tallowmere hasn't answered him in a month."

He glances at your lute, and something stirs in his face. "There was a girl once. Years back, before I served there. The old cook still tells it. A storyteller came to Thornhallow with a girl who sang at my lord's table, and my lord wept at the last verse and gave her a ribbon. Was that... you?"

He doesn't wait for an answer. "If you came to my lord as the storyteller's apprentice, with a word or a token only that circle would know, he might listen. Thornhallow's a day's walk downriver, and the assessor comes in three days. I leave at dawn on a hired mule. I could say nothing of you, or I could say a friend of Master Tallowmere is coming."

The night is quiet, and the market lamps are going out one by one behind you. You hold a copy of Corvin's draft, a tin of his seal impressions, and a ledger of his lies in your psalter binding. Whatever you choose to do about Verrel's third descent, you can do it with all of them.

What does Wren decide? Tell Tobin to speak of you or stay silent, whether you'll walk to Thornhallow, and whether you want to settle in for the night in Marrowford first.

DM: what was the ribbon Lord Edric gave the girl at his table, do I remember its color, and do I still have it?

DM: Tobin nods fast, relief plain on his face. "A friend of Master Tallowmere is coming. Nothing else. Not your name, not a word to Hale." He says it twice more under his breath, as if learning a verse.

The Drowned Candle is a low, crooked inn on the near bank, with a lantern in a bottle-glass window. It smells of boiled eel and peat smoke. For a silver piece you get floor space by the hearth in the common room, and for six more copper a bowl of fish stew and a heel of bread. Tobin takes a straw pallet against the wall near you, one hand never far from his knife. That's 1 sp 6 cp spent, and you have 14 gp 7 sp 4 cp left.

As for the ribbon, that's your past, so it's your call rather than mine. Tobin only repeated the cook's tale, and I won't decide what you remember. Tell me, if you like:
- Do you remember that night at Thornhallow at all?
- What color was the ribbon, and do you still have it? It could be the token that gets you a hearing, or it could be a thing you never thought twice about.

Once you've said, I'll move you through the night and into the morning road.

== ROUND 9 ==
PLAYER: I remember it faintly: a high-ceilinged hall, a fire, a weeping lord, and a deep green velvet ribbon that Corvin pocketed and never gave me. But I kept a scrap of it, because I slit the hem of my old caravan cloak to hide the end of it, and I've worn it there ever since without thinking much of it.

I settle in by the hearth, eat my stew, and keep one eye half-open on Tobin's pallet through the night.

Tobin is already awake, tying his bundle, and a hired mule stamps in the yard. He gives you a nod and a small, nervous half-smile. "A friend of Master Tallowmere is coming," he mutters, then slips out the door to ride ahead.

The river road to Thornhallow runs downriver from the bridge, a day's walk through farmland and willow-banks. It's six in the morning, you have 14 gp 7 sp 4 cp, and the assessor is two days and some hours away.

Corvin's driver and wagon are still on the square. Corvin himself is somewhere upriver. You can head straight down the river road, or take a few minutes in Marrowford first for supplies, a better lockpick set, or anything else you want.

The river road runs down the Marrow's north bank through willow-banks, wheat fields gone gold, and stone farmsteads with smoke rising. You keep your eyes on the road, on every cart, every tradesman, every man whose smile lingers a beat too long. You see nothing. For a moment you wonder whether that's good luck or just poor looking, and you can't be sure which. Your Perception check was a natural 1, and nothing you saw tells you either way.

You walk through the day with your lute on your back, stopping to eat on a milestone and refill your waterskin at a roadside trough. The sun is slanting low when the road tops a rise and the land opens out. Below sits Thornhallow: a small walled manor on a bend of the river, grey stone with a slate roof, a gatehouse flying a green and white pennant, and a village of thatched cottages leaning against its wall like children against a parent. Smoke curls from the chimneys. It is a quarter past three.

There's a guard at the gate, and the manor is quiet. Somewhere inside, Tobin has arrived, and a worried lord is waiting for a friend of Corvin Tallowmere.

What does Wren do?

DM: Your Persuasion check was a 13, enough. The guard is a stout, grey-whiskered man in a green tabard. He hears you through, chews on the word "ribbon," and his face changes. "Wait here," he says, and he sends a boy running across the yard.

Twenty minutes later you're led through the gate and across a cobbled courtyard where hens scatter, then in through a side door and up a narrow stair to a study. It's a plain room, stone-walled, with a lit fire despite the warm day and a heavy desk covered in unsealed papers. Under the window sits an iron-bound chest, just as Tobin said.

Lord Edric Verrel stands when you enter. He is a narrow, stooped man of perhaps fifty, with ink on his cuffs and shadows under his eyes. Tobin hovers at his shoulder, pale and watching you.

"I was told a friend of Master Tallowmere was coming," Lord Edric says. His voice is thin and careful. "Tobin tells me you remember a ribbon. Many people could have learned that from a servant's gossip. Show me, if you would, what you have."

Beside the desk, in a shadowed corner, a man you haven't seen before sits quietly with a ledger open on his knee. He is neat, middle-aged and softly spoken, and he gives you a courteous, perfectly warm smile. He hasn't been introduced.

What does Wren do?

Then you turn to the man in the corner, and your Insight check, a 21, tells you what the smile hides. It hasn't changed in all the time you've watched it. It's fixed, practised, and a little too exact. When the ribbon touched the desk his eyes went to the iron chest under the window, then to the door, then back, quick as a bird. He knew you were coming, and he knows who you are.

"Master Hale, steward and clerk of the household," the man says, rising with a small bow. His voice is soft and pleasant. "I keep my lord's accounts. And you, mistress, I gather, are the storyteller's apprentice. I must say my lord has been quite beside himself. Perhaps I should remain, to keep a record of whatever is agreed."

Lord Edric hesitates, glancing from you to his steward, and his hand closes over the ribbon. He's a man who has leaned on others for a long time. "Hale... leave us. A moment. I will call you."

"My lord," Hale says gently, "the matter of the assessor touches the whole house..."

"I said leave us."

Hale inclines his head. He goes slowly, and the door closes behind him with a soft, exact click. You hear his footsteps retreat along the corridor, but not quite all the way.

Tobin hovers by the hearth, miserable. Lord Edric looks at you with a lost, hopeful face. "Tell me, child. Is the charter lost? Can it be mended? What did Corvin do?"

You have a copy of the draft, the ledger in your psalter, and a tin of seal impressions. Hale may be listening at the door. What does Wren say or do?

DM: You kneel on the flagstones and set the little brass lantern in his knotted hands. You tell him what it is, where it came from, and that you've never known if what it holds is true. For a long moment Brother Osric says nothing. His thumb moves over the worn etching on the lid, a lamp with rays, and his lips shape a prayer you almost know.

He reads it aloud, and the words are plain: *"Let none who bear this lamp walk unlit. Let the false word fall from their tongue, and the true one light their road."* His eyes are wet. "Whoever wrote that meant it. It's a true blessing, child, and you carried it a long way without knowing."

You don't know what to do with your hands, so you hold still. Tobin looks away.

Osric returns the parchment to its case, folds your fingers around it, and gets to his feet with a groan. "Come."

In the vestry, from a chest under the altar stone, he lifts a thick register bound in cracked calfskin and a second, thinner copy written in his own cramped hand. He finds the page without searching, because he has read it many times. The entry is short. Aldous Verrel, grandsire of Lord Edric, married in this chapel by plain rite to Maud Fenn, miller's daughter of Thornhallow. There is no Aldermere in it, no great house, and no blood of the old line. Corvin changed the bride's name and her family.

"The manor was granted to the Verrels by plain deed," Osric says. "The descent was only ever for precedence and the tax-right. The land is theirs without it." He hesitates. "Master Hale asked to see this. I refused him."

It is five-twelve in the evening. You have the truth in hand, a day and a half before Dame Crowe arrives, and you can't be sure Hale hasn't another way into the chapel.

Hale lets out a slow breath. "My lord," he says quietly, "I'll be plain, since the girl has made plainness fashionable. Corvin Tallowmere's ledger is a record of the forgeries he sold to three houses. It would ruin Verrel in the right hands, and Dame Crowe's would be the right hands. This girl stole it from her master and has carried it here. I have been trying, for your sake, to take it from a thief and burn it before it harmed you." His eyes slide to you. "Ask her if it's true that she took it. Ask her what she means to do with it."

Tobin makes a small, frightened sound by the hearth. The kitchen has gathered quietly in the passage, listening. Lord Edric looks at you, and his voice is gentle, almost afraid. "Child. Is it so? Do you have Corvin's ledger?"

The lie that would be easiest isn't what any of them are waiting for. What does Wren say or do?

== ROUND 24 ==
PLAYER: "Yes, my lord, I have it, and I'll not lie to you about that." I take the psalter from my pack, work the binding open, and slide out only the Verrel pages. "I took the ledger because I couldn't stomach what Corvin had done, and I came to find the people he'd cheated. This is your house's page. Burn it in the hearth, my lord, in front of everyone, because it's yours to burn, not Hale's."

I close the psalter again and look at Hale. "The other pages belong to two other houses, and I'll decide what's right for them myself, not by a steward's say-so. But I notice he wanted it burned *before* you saw it, and you're the one it concerns."

DM: You don't flinch. You take out the psalter, work the stitched binding open with a thumbnail, and slide free a single folded leaf, then close the book again in plain view of the whole passage. The room stays utterly still.

"This is your house's page, my lord. It's yours to burn, not Hale's."

Lord Edric unfolds it. Corvin's neat hand lists dates, the sum paid and the descent altered. In the margin, in a smaller and different ink, run neat little initials and tallies: *H. — 2s. in the pound.* He reads them through, and you watch his shoulders go slowly straight.

"Two shillings in the pound," he says. "Out of my own purse."

He crosses to the hearth and drops the page onto the coals. It curls, browns, blooms briefly orange, and is gone. No one speaks. Then he turns to the doorway. "Master Hale will wait in the cellar store with the gate-guard at the door until the assessor comes. Nobody will lay a hand on him. Nobody will leave him without bread and water." His voice cracks only a little. "Take him."

Hale goes without a word, the smile folded away like a handkerchief. In the confusion Garrow is gone, over the dovecote wall into the dusk. Tobin thinks he went toward the Marrowford road, probably to carry word upriver to Corvin.

Lord Edric sits down heavily. He looks at you for a long time. "I'll tell Dame Crowe the plain truth," he says, "with Osric's name beside mine. If I lose the precedence, I lose it. A miller's granddaughter's bread was never a shameful thing." Then, softly: "What do I owe you, child?"

You have gained 100 XP, bringing you to 100 of 300. It's a quarter past seven on Day 2. Corvin is somewhere upriver and may learn that his forgery has failed, and two other houses in your psalter are still owed the truth.

What does Wren do?

"Looking for the shrine-wagon, love? Gone at dawn. A man in a brown coat came in on a lathered horse, shouted at the driver, and off they went up the Ashby road like the river was on fire." She sniffs. "Strongbox and all."

So Garrow got word and the wagon moved, and the wagon is now on the road to Ashby Weir, the same road you meant to take, an hour or two of start ahead of you and a team of horses to your mule. It's half past one on Day 3.

*Wren, my girl.*

*You took the wrong book. I'm not angry. A father isn't angry when a clever child proves she's clever. Come to the ford at Ashby Weir at dusk on the fifth day, alone, and bring it. We'll talk like family and see what can be mended, you and I, before someone less gentle than I am gets the idea of reading it for themselves.*

*One thing more, for the road. That curl of parchment in your little brass lantern is not my work. I never wrote it. Ask yourself who did, and why I let you carry it all this way.*

*C.* There's a tiny drawn lantern beneath the initial.

The room feels quiet in a strange way. He knew about the reliquary, and about the parchment. He's telling you it's real, and that he let you keep it, and nothing about that note is the voice of a man who thinks he has lost.

The fifth day is two days away. The wagon is somewhere on the Ashby road, and Lady Thistlewood's gate is a day's ride past this fork. Marta has set a bowl of barley broth by the hearth for you.

DM: You tie Biscuit among the lobster pots and knock on the green door. Nothing, then a shuffle, then the bolt. The door opens a hand's width on a face like a dried apple, with white whiskers and bright, watery eyes. "Who's that? Is it the tax-man?"

"A traveler of the Lantern-Bearer, sent by Mother Pell, who says you keep the best memory on the river." You hold up the reliquary so the dying sun catches its rays, and then the sketch. Your Persuasion check was a 13, and the eyes sharpen.

"The crooked flame," he whispers. The door opens, and he draws you into a smoky room that smells of tar and pipe-weed. He sits you by the hearth and takes the sketch in a shaking hand, and for a moment you think his wits have drifted off. Then they come back like a boat on a tide.

"Elowen," he says. "Elowen Ashgrove. A road-priest of the Lantern, proper, with a lamp-staff and a looped hand. She came through the shrine twenty years back with a babe wrapped in a green blanket, hungry and cold, and she asked me to cut the book's pages for that season. I said it was a sin, and she said it was a mercy. So I did, with her own knife." He wipes his eye. "She left the lamp over the door, and a curl of blessing in a little brass case, and said she was going upriver to Ashby Weir, to a house she trusted. And that was the last of her. I never saw her again."

His gaze settles on your face for a long moment. "A storyteller came asking after her the next spring. Silver-tongued. Too many questions. I told him nothing, and he went off sulking. That would have been your Master Tallowmere, would it?"

He says her surname the same way the world has said yours since you were small. *Ashgrove.* You have no memory of a name before it, and the old man is watching your face for what you'll do with it.

Dusk is over an hour away. You could ask him what house she trusted, what became of the baby, or who else knew. What does Wren do?

DM: You lay the scrap of green velvet on his knee and tell him to take his time. The old man doesn't touch it at first. He looks at it for a long while with his mouth working, and the fire pops, and a gull cries on the weir. You wait.

"Green velvet," he says at last, very softly. "Aye. The blanket was edged in it, deep as moss, a good bolt, a noble's cloth. It was too fine for a priest's baby. I remember thinking that." He lifts the scrap with one thumb and forefinger as if it might bruise. "She said she was going to the lady on the spur. Old Lady Agnes Thistlewood, the grandmother of the house, may she rest. She was kind. Good to the road. Elowen said, 'Agnes will keep her safe till I can come back.'" His voice thins. "But she never came back, as far as I ever heard, and I don't know what Lady Agnes did with the child. Only..."

He frowns, hunting for the thread. "Some months after, a storyteller came down the Marrowford road with a little girl of about that age on his cart. Said he found her at a roadside shrine, wrapped in a blanket. Pretty talker. I thought nothing of it then." He looks up at you and doesn't say the rest, because he doesn't need to.

"Lady Maren rules the spur now, her daughter," he adds. "A fair woman, but afraid of the village over the ferry-toll, and not much liked. She was only a girl herself then. She might remember, or she might not."

The fire burns low. Outside, the light has turned gold and slanting over the ford. It's ten past six, and dusk is an hour away. Corvin's meeting is tomorrow at dusk, and the Thistlewood house is on the spur above the village.

What does Wren do?

DM: She reads the leaf by the firelight, once, then again, her ink-stained thumb moving along Corvin's neat hand. When she looks up there isn't surprise in her face, only a deep weariness, as of a debt long expected. "I wondered," she says. "I was twenty-four when she came," she says. "A road-priest, thin as a heron, with a baby in a blanket the colour of a forest. My mother was sick that year, and she took them in without a question. The child slept in my own old cradle by that fire for three months, until the road-priest went out one evening and didn't come back." Her voice catches. "Then a man arrived, a storyteller, charming, silver-tongued, with a letter in the priest's hand asking for the child to be given to him. My mother was ill and she believed it. She gave him the baby, and the key to a coffer the priest had left behind 'for the girl when she's grown.' I watched him ride away with her." She presses her lips together. "I've wondered for twenty years if I should have said something."

She rises. "The coffer is still here. Mother wouldn't send it away, and the key went with the storyteller. Come."

She leads you down a stair to a small stone strongroom, and by the light of her candle you see it on a shelf: a little iron-bound coffer scratched with a lantern and the letters *E.A.* It is no bigger than a bread-box. Locked.

You've heard Corvin's name linked to this twice in a day. He has the key, and he'll be at the ford at dusk tomorrow.

DM: You kneel on the cold stones with the candle on the shelf above you and turn the little brass lantern in your hands. Your Investigation check was an 18. Under the base, hidden among the cast rays, a boss of metal stands a hair proud, and it isn't a decoration. It has teeth. It's a key.

You hold your breath and fit it to the coffer's keyhole. It slides in with a small, soft click, as if it had been waiting twenty years, and turns. Lady Maren catches her breath behind you. The iron key Corvin carried off was never going to fit.

The lid rises. Inside, on a bed of faded green velvet, lie three things: a small silver pendant shaped like a lantern, a folded paper sealed with plain wax and marked with a lamp-staff, and a single letter in a looped hand you know from the parchment in your reliquary. It's addressed on the outside: *To my wren.*

You read it kneeling, with a hand over your mouth. It isn't long. Elowen writes that Corvin Tallowmere was her own apprentice, that he turned her blessings into pedigrees and sold the road's mercy to men who wanted it for their vanity, and that she meant to confront him. If she did not return, she wrote, he would come for the child with false letters and a pleasant face. *Light the road, don't sell it. You'll know what that means when you're old enough to be ashamed.* Beneath the signature, a lantern with a crooked flame. The folded paper is a commission, naming you heir to her lamp-staff and her calling, if you want it.

You've gained the letter, the pendant, and the commission, and learned more than you came for. It's ten past eight on Day 4. Corvin is a day's wait away at the ford, and he knows it is true.

Lady Maren waits quietly, hand on the door. What does Wren do?

Someone else shouts that the Thistlewoods should refund every penny. A tankard flies. Two of Lady Maren's men-at-arms, Alder and Kit by their badges, take you by the elbows and hustle you out the back as the room swells into a roar. By afternoon it's across the village that the ford tale is a lie, the toll is cursed, and that Lady Maren must answer for it. It's not what you meant, but it isn't gone back into the bottle, either.

In the afternoon you scout the ford. Your Perception check was a 6, and it tells you only that the stones are wet and the willows thick and quiet. There's nothing to see. The best place, you decide, is the broad, flat stone midway across, within hail of Hob's cottage and the bank where a few villagers have gathered, uneasily, to watch. Alder and Kit go quietly into the willows with Lady Maren's blessing. Hob Crannock sits by his green door with a pipe, as if he's merely taking the air.

You eat a little, you check your rapier, and you smooth the pendant at your throat. The sun drops behind the spur and the river turns copper. It's five in the evening, dusk is an hour off. In the green and gold light, down the Ashby road, comes the creak of a wagon, a painted lantern swinging on its side, and a lean man walking before it in a brown coat.

He says something quiet to Garrow, who stops where he is with a glance at the willows, and then, alone, he steps onto the stones. "Wren," he says, softly enough that only the water carries it beyond you. "You've grown. Even your scowl has grown." A rueful, warm smile. "You've been busy. I heard about Verrel, and about the common room. A wonderful speech, I'm told, if poorly received."

DM: The rapier's point had barely touched his collar when Corvin's eyes flicked over your shoulder. Your Intimidation check was a 3, and the driver took no heed of it. You see Corvin's mouth begin to curve, and he says nothing. He doesn't give the order. The bolt strikes before you can turn.

It's a critical hit. Eleven points of damage drop you off the stone and into the cold ford, and the world goes grey at the edges. You hear shouting, the splash of boots, water in your ears. One bolt more flies past you and misses. Then Alder's spear goes wide, and Kit's finds the driver, and he folds beside Garrow in the shallows.

You lie at the bottom of the dark. Twice you hear Alder's voice and Hob's wheeze over you, hands pressing at your wound and failing, and Kit swearing in a language you don't know. You make your death saves in the dark, one ten, then a thirteen before it, and the next round something bright and fierce answers in you: a natural 20. You come up gasping into the lamplight with one hit point and a mouthful of river.

Corvin is gone. When you turn your head, the flat stone is empty, a wet footprint drying on it. One of the wagon's horses is missing from the picket line, and the dusk has swallowed him. The wagon stands on the bank with its painted lantern, abandoned, and the strongbox sits on its tailboard, still locked.

You are alive. It's nearly six now, on Day 5. You're at 1 hit point, 1 spell slot left, and 225 XP, 75 short of the next level. I gave you 50 XP for the fight. Garrow and the driver are dead, and the villagers are gathered quietly on the bank, looking at you with a different face than they wore at noon. Lady Maren is hurrying down from the spur.

Corvin said "Saltmarsh," and you still have the ledger in your psalter, two leaves for Thistlewood and Dray, and a night of rest ahead.

DM: Alder and Kit haul you up the bank like a netted fish, and you cough up what feels like half the Marrow. You manage a croaky "Thank you, both, and you, Hob," and Hob only snorts around his pipe, wiping his eyes with a knuckle. Lady Maren arrives at a run with a lantern and a blanket, takes one look at you, and says nothing at all for a moment. Then she orders a litter.

Before they carry you off, you send Kit to the driver, and he comes back with a dead man's belt-pouch. Inside is a small iron key, a few coppers, and a scrap of vellum folded small. Hob takes the wagon watch with a rug over his knees and his pipe, and Alder stays at his side.

You sleep in the same small room under the eaves, in a clean bed with a jug of water and a heel of bread. It's a long, heavy sleep, and when you wake the window is full of white morning. You're whole again: all your hit points and your spell slots, and Bardic Inspiration back at three. The night has passed without trouble.

At seven you go down to the yard with Kit. The wagon stands by the tithe-barn, its painted lantern faded in the daylight, and the strongbox sits on its tailboard. The little iron key fits. Inside, packed in straw, lie a good set of lock-picks and tools, a full forgery kit in a lacquered case, a purse of sixty gold pieces in mixed coin, and one slip of paper in Corvin's neat hand: *The Salt Gull, Saltmarsh, the ninth.*

That's Day 9. You've pocketed the money, the kit and the tools, and the note. The tools you wanted at the tinker's are now yours, 74 gold and some silver in all.

Lady Maren stands at your elbow, her hands clasped. "The village is quiet this morning," she says. "They saw you take a bolt for the truth. I think they'll listen now." She hesitates. "Will you ride for Saltmarsh? Sir Corwen Dray is a hard man, I hear, and proud. You'll want something to carry in front of you."

You have three days before the ninth. Saltmarsh is some five days' ride by the river road, so you won't make it by Day 9 without hard riding and a lucky road. You're at 225 XP, 75 short of level 2.

What does Wren do?

I turn to Kit with a grin. "And if Alder can spare you for a week, I'd gladly buy your spear on the road, a lady needs a second pair of eyes when she's chasing a man who nearly watched her die."

DM: Lady Maren doesn't answer at once. She looks at the wagon, at the faded lantern, at Alder leaning on his spear, and then at you. "A horse, yes. My father's grey, Ember, who's too good for this village and bored with it. And a letter." She goes into the manor and returns within a quarter of an hour with a sealed square of vellum, and she hands it over with both hands, as if it were heavier than it looked. She has written it in her own square hand, naming you her friend and Elowen Ashgrove's daughter, "with every claim on the house of Thistlewood that her mother had, and gladly given."

Biscuit goes back to the stable with the promise that Lord Edric's stable-boy will fetch him when the dust settles. Ember is a tall grey with a white blaze and an opinion of himself, and he regards you the way a cat regards a puddle.

Then she turns to Kit. "I'll not order it. I'll ask." Kit says nothing for a moment, one thumb in her sword-belt. She looks at the wagon, at the ford, at you. "Aye," she says at last. "I've never been past the weir. Time I saw where the river goes." Alder claps her on the shoulder, and Lady Maren pays her a month's wage in advance without being asked. Kit stows her shield on her back, shoulders a pack, and gives you a brief, dry nod, no speech.

Kit is a human fighter, a solid companion with chain mail, a shield, a longsword, a light crossbow and a steady head. She has her own character sheet now and takes her turns as herself in a fight, on your orders if you give them.

It's quarter to eight on Day 6. Saltmarsh is days away, and the Salt Gull meeting is on Day 9. The river road runs down past Marrowford and Thornhallow, and you can leave now, or stop on the way.

Tobin and Osric are safe, and Osric has taken to reading the register aloud to anyone who stands still.

Crowe also left a message for you: she wants the storyteller Corvin Tallowmere, and the ledger, brought to her. She says a girl who confesses unprompted is worth hearing, and that she will be at the Marrowford assize at month's end.

You are both at exhaustion level 2: disadvantage on ability checks, and speed halved. That's the rule, and it's why the last miles drag. You stop at a lock-keeper's cottage in the afternoon for a few hours' sleep and some bread, then limp on through Day 8's evening and dawn along the widening river as the air turns to salt and gull-cry.

It's a quarter to ten on the morning of the ninth. Saltmarsh spreads below you on a grey estuary: slate roofs, drying nets, a stone quay, the squat bulk of Saltmarsh Keep on a rise to the north. Banners of white and blue hang from the keep. In the town below, a bell has begun to toll the hour, and a small crowd is moving toward a hall by the quay marked with the Vantry scales.

The Salt Gull is a tarred inn at the quay's end, its sign a swinging gull. You're stiff, tired, and at a disadvantage on any skill check, with the contract sealing sometime today.

She isn't a villain. She's frightened, and she's given you the one thing she can: the hour. It's twenty past ten. At noon the contract is sealed, and whoever is behind the brass lock has until then to be somewhere else.

The Vantry elder is whispering furiously to his son. The clerk in black gloves has gone white and is edging toward the side of the dais, his case clutched to his chest. At the wall, Kit has taken one slow step off the brick and now stands between him and the way out.

The arbiter hasn't looked away from him. "The seal stays unset," she says. "Sir Corwen, sit. The page, mistress."

Then you run. With your speed halved by exhaustion it's a stiff, graceless run, but you take the low step in a clumsy hop and land at the dais's edge, five feet short of him, breathing hard. That is as far as you can get this turn. It's close enough.

Corvin stands with his hand still on the latch and his back to you. For a moment his shoulders do nothing at all. Then he turns, and the gentle teacher's mask is gone, and what's under it is only a tired, cold, calculating man.

Corvin's eyes flick to the door, then to the guards, then back to you. The latch ring is under his hand, and he could try to haul it open again. One look says he is weighing it.

He glances once at the corner of the lane where Kit is hidden, plainly knowing she's there, and then back at you. His hands are open and empty. He doesn't seem in any hurry at all.

The spit is a long bar of pale shingle, with the Gullspit light at its landward-facing end, a squat dark tower of fitted stone with a cracked lantern-room on top. Your Perception check was only a 6, with disadvantage. You can't be sure whether anyone has gone ashore, or whether the Heron's Tithe is still anchored off the south side. Gulls wheel around the tower, and the water is slapping the stones.

There is a boat-landing on the seaward face, a rough stair of weed-slick steps. The door at its top hangs ajar. Dunn looks at the tower and then at you. "I'll wait off the point," he says, "but I'll not tie up. Folk don't come back from there, or didn't. It's quarter past seven."

You have two spell slots, 17 hit points, Kit's sword, and a lamp-staff that opens like a lantern.

What does Wren do?

At the open door I stop and listen first, my hand on the rapier but not drawn, then call quietly up the dark stair, "Elowen Ashgrove? It's Wren. I carry your staff."

DM: You step onto the weedy landing with Kit at your back, hold the lamp-staff upright so the brass lantern catches the light, and stop at the open door with your hand on the rapier hilt. You listen. Your Perception check is a 1 on the die, a total of 3 with disadvantage, and you hear nothing but wind, gulls and your own heart. The stair is dark and smells of cold ash.

You call up it, quietly: "Elowen Ashgrove? It's Wren. I carry your staff."

For a long moment there's silence. Then a cracked old voice answers from high above. "Elowen's been dead sixteen years, girl. But she said you'd come." A slow, uneven tread begins to descend. A gaunt, white-bearded man appears on the stair in a patched coat, a stub of tallow candle in one hand. He holds it up and looks at the lantern in your hand, and his weathered face crumples. "Brann Tolliver. I kept the light, and I kept her. She's under the north wall. She left a word for you, and I've held it since."

He doesn't get any further. Kit, who has not taken her eyes off the shingle, says in a low flat voice, "Wren. Behind us." Her passive Perception caught what yours missed. Along the spit's south shingle, six or seven hundred feet off, a longboat has grounded, and five figures are coming up the stones in a loose line: Sparrow's brown coat, three hands with boarding-pikes, and in the middle, a tall figure in a grey cloak, hooded, walking as though the spit belonged to him.

Brann has gone grey. "That's the one who came for her," he whispers. "Sixteen years ago, in a grey cloak. I thought he was dead."

They'll reach the beacon in a minute or two. The stair rises behind Brann, narrow and defensible. Dunn's skiff is already pulling away off the point, and the north landing is empty at your feet. It's twenty-two minutes past seven.

What does Wren do?

DM: You haul the door shut, and Kit wrestles an old salt-crate against it with a grunt. Brann Tolliver hurries up ahead of you with his candle, breathless, and says Elowen's word as you climb, in the flat voice of a man who has said it to himself a thousand times:

"Tell my wren the lamp in the tower answers only to the lantern-foot. Light it when the storm comes. Do not let him make you his."

You take your place two steps up the narrow stair, where you can see the door's foot over the crate. Kit plants herself in the doorway, shield up, with the stair close at her back and the stone walls so tight that only one pike at a time can come through. Brann climbs on to the lantern-room above, muttering. The lamp-staff's brass lantern glints in your hand.

Boots crunch on the shingle outside, and stop. For a few heartbeats there's nothing but gull-cry. Then a smooth, unhurried voice speaks through the planks.

"Miss Ashgrove. I won't shout, there's no need." The tone is gentle and cultured, with something else under it, patient as a tide. "You're quite safe in there, for the moment. So is Master Tallowmere, in his cellar. I could have him out of it by noon, and nobody the wiser." A pause. "Open the door. Hand me the staff and the old man, and everyone goes home. Elowen would not have wanted blood on her grave."

Sparrow's soft laugh comes from somewhere nearby. A pike-butt knocks twice on the planks, almost politely.

Kit hasn't moved. She says, without turning her head, "Your call."

You've two spell slots, three uses of Bardic Inspiration, and a minute at most. What does Wren do?

I'll give you ten breaths." The pike-butt knocks once more. "Then my men will see what a salt-crate is worth against a boarding-pike."

Over your shoulder you whisper up the stair to Brann. He peers down at you from the turn, candle shaking. "The glass has been falling since dawn," he whispers back. "There'll be a squall off the estuary by afternoon, hours yet. As for waking the lamp early..." He swallows. "She never told me how. Only that the lantern-foot opens it, and that it wasn't to be lit for nothing."

The ten breaths are going. The stair is narrow and steep, the door is barred with a crate that won't hold a determined push, and Brann's lantern-room is above with a trapdoor that might bar it. Outside there are at least five of them, and one with the cold patient voice, whom you can't see.

Kit's sword is half out. She is breathing slowly through her nose. What does Wren do?

Elowen came to the spit alone in the autumn sixteen years ago, already fevered and thin as a lath. A hooded man landed and talked with her alone on the shingle, and Brann heard her call him "Aldous". She told him, "It is not yours to light." He left furious. She died that winter of the fever, and asked to be buried with her face to the lamp. Brann kept the light dark by her word and waited for the lantern-foot.

"I thought him dead," he says, looking out across the water at the grey sail, now very small. "He had a way of looking at the lamp that I've never seen on a man since, as if it owed him money."

  [game] notebook: updated The Gullspit Light
DM: Out of the story first, on both your questions.

On the name: I'll be straight with you. I picked "Aldous" for the Grey Cloak without checking it against Brother Osric's register, so it wasn't a planted clue. It does collide with Lord Edric's long-dead grandsire, and you're right to notice. I'll leave it open, as a possible lead and not a promise. Aldous Verrel would be far too old to be your Grey Cloak, so if there's a link, it's an alias or a family tie, and you'd find it by asking Lord Edric, Osric, or the Thornhallow records. It could also just be a common river-country name. Chase it if you like, and you'll learn whatever the world gives you.

On the lamp, here's my ruling, which I'll apply the same way every time. The great lamp burns only while the reliquary's lantern-foot is seated in its socket. If you take the foot, the lamp goes dark. If you put it back, it lights again. You'd need the foot to open Elowen's other hiding places, and the paper says it is the proof of your claim.

Now the story. Brann shakes his head slowly. "No surname, no house. Only 'Aldous', said the way she said it, like a thing she'd known a long time." He adds, with an effort, "The ship had a grey sail, much like that one. That's all."

Kit has finished laying out the dead. She stands up, brushing shingle off her knees, and looks at the sky, which has begun to cloud over from the west. "Weather," she says. Brann's squall is on its way.

So the lamp is lit and has to be a choice. You can leave the reliquary in the socket, so the light burns on and Brann keeps it, though you'd have no lantern-foot for any other doors. Or you can take the foot and let the lamp go dark again.

Dunn is ready to take the dead and the living back to Saltmarsh, though he mutters about the weather. What does Wren do?

  [game] notebook: updated Corvin Tallowmere
DM: The arbiter doesn't ask why. She looks at the rain on the shutters and at your face, then sends Pell down with you. Pell stays at the cellar door with his halberd grounded and his eyes on the ceiling, a man who is hearing nothing.

Corvin sits on a cot with a blanket over his knees. The black gloves are gone, and his hands look older without them. He sees the folded statement and smiles, rueful and tired. "You came back. I did wonder."

You read him his own line. He doesn't flinch.

"A dead man's name." He turns it over like a coin. "I heard it once, on a wet night in a roadhouse, when he'd had too much of the good wine and thought I was too far gone to listen. He said, 'It's only borrowed, Corvin. I'm wearing it until I've earned it back.' And later: the real Aldous was 'a Verrel by blood, and no friend to the Verrels' paper.' That's all he ever gave me. I think the man he is was never meant to have a name, and he's trying on one that has a claim attached." He spreads his hands. "Whether that makes him a bastard, a cousin, or a thief, I never learned. I swear it on the Lantern, whatever you think that's worth."

You ask about yourself, and the room gets quieter.

"No," he says. "It was never the whole of it." He looks at the wall for a while. "I didn't buy you, Wren. I told the caravan I did because it made a better tale. You were four, perhaps five. Elowen had left you at a roadside shrine-house on the lower Marrow, with a woman who took coin to keep you and ask nothing. I found it. I paid the woman more than Elowen had, and I took you." His voice stays level. "I told myself it was to keep Aldous from finding you, and that was partly true. The rest is that a child with Elowen's face, kept near the lantern-foot, was worth keeping. I won't dress that up. I raised you well enough, and I used you, and both are so."

You press for the name of the shrine-house and the woman. He shakes his head slowly. "Not from this cot. Get me out of this cellar into Dame Crowe's keeping, where the road is open to me and the man who smiles can't reach me, and I'll draw you the map myself." A thin, dry smile. "I know how that sounds. Your mother's staff is in your hands, and you've still not trusted me, which is the best thing I ever taught you."

You study him as he speaks. Your Insight check is a 13 (with disadvantage from exhaustion), and he doesn't give you anything to hold. Every word sounds like the truth, and it sounds the same way his best tales always did. You can't tell which parts are true and which are his craft.

It is a little after one. The storm is still grumbling overhead, Kit is asleep upstairs, and Pell is very carefully not listening. What does Wren do?

== ROUND 100 ==
PLAYER: I stand, tuck the statement away, and look at him steadily. "You're not getting out of that cellar, Corvin, not by a bargain. Dame Crowe holds the assize at Marrowford at month's end, and I'll make sure you reach it alive and in one piece, because I want you to stand there and say all of it under oath, including how you came by me."

DM: You climb back to the Salt Gull, peel off your wet boots, and sleep through the afternoon by the fire with Kit snoring across the room. It isn't a long rest. You wake stiff and still weary, with no slots back and exhaustion 1 still on you. The storm has blown itself thin to a steady drizzle.

It's in Corvin's own neat hand. He has drawn the lower Marrow as a ribbon, with Saltmarsh at its mouth and a mark a day's ride upriver on the east bank. *Reedlow Landing. The Rushlight House, a ferry-stop shrine. Goodwife Annis Marl kept the child through one winter and a spring. Say Elowen's name only after she has said yours.* Below that, in smaller writing: *She may be dead. If so, her niece keeps the ferry. I would not go by the main road.*

You try to check it against what you hold. You go through Elowen's letter and the strip from the staff, and your Investigation check is only a 4, with disadvantage from exhaustion. Neither paper names Reedlow or the Rushlight House, and you can't tell whether the hand is honest or just very good. The map neither confirms nor contradicts anything. It's a day's ride for something that may be a true lead, a trap, or a long way to nowhere.

That leaves you with choices over supper. You could ride to Reedlow Landing in the morning after your long rest finishes at about four. You could fetch the reliquary from the spit first, since Brann has the lamp lit and the foot is the proof of your claim. You could wait here for the arbiter's rider to return from Thornhallow with word on "Aldous". Or you could do something else entirely. Kit pushes her bowl away and says, "Your call. I'd like to sleep through the night before any of it."

It's a little after six in the evening. What does Wren decide?

DM: Out of the story first, on your question. My ruling is that the day count and the calendar date match, so "month's end" is the 30th and Day 10 is the 10th. That leaves twenty days to Dame Crowe's assize. Marrowford is about four days' ride from Saltmarsh by the river road. Reedlow Landing lies a day upriver on the way, so it is on your road and you'd lose almost nothing by going. Your three-day round trip fits easily. It is a little after six now. The rain has stopped, and the sky over the estuary is washed pale. Out on the spit the Gullspit light still burns white, small and steady, with no grey sail near it. Bess has hot porridge and the grey horse Ember is saddled in the yard. The back road runs inland along the dike, longer than the main one, and Corvin warned against the main road.

DM: You eat the porridge, load the saddlebags, and take the dike road with Ember's hooves soft in the wet clay. The country opens flat and silver on both sides, with reed-beds and drowned willows, and the sky is huge after the rain. You ride watchful, with your eyes on every hedge and gap. Your Perception check is an 8, and you find nothing worth a second look. There are no brown coats and no grey cloaks, only herons, a boy driving geese, and a cart that turns off at a farm.

You teach Kit the new verse as you go. She mouths it twice before she'll hum it, flat and surprisingly true, and by noon she's muttering the words to herself while she watches the hedgerows. "Not bad," you tell her. "Don't tell anyone," she says. It is the longest conversation she has offered in days.

You stop once to water the horses and eat bread and cheese in the lee of a dike-mill. The back road takes its time, winding around marsh and drainage ditches. The sun is low and gold when the land begins to rise toward a belt of alders and a flat brown stretch of river.

Reedlow Landing is a handful of low houses, a reed-thatched ferry shed, a stone jetty, and a flat-bottomed ferry tied against the current. A little way back from the water, on the old road, stands a small shrine-house of grey stone, plain as a cattle-shed. A tin lantern with a rush-wick hangs over its door, unlit. Two or three villagers eye you over their doors, and a woman sweeping a step stops to watch you come.

It is a little after five. The jetty and the ferry-shed are on your right, the shrine on the left, and the sun has about two hours left. You've three spell slots, three Bardic Inspirations, and Corvin's warning: *Say Elowen's name only after she has said yours.* What does Wren do?

DM: You hand Kit the reins, show your empty hands, and walk up the wet path alone. The woman with the broom has stopped sweeping. She is broad and weathered, with forearms like a ferryman's and a face that gives nothing away. She leans the broom against the wall and looks you over, from the rapier to the lamp-staff to your face. Then she looks at your face a second time.

"Annis Marl," she says slowly, "is my aunt. She's abed, and she's old, and she doesn't take strangers." Her mouth tightens. "I'm Tamsin. I keep the ferry. Say your business plain, and then say your name, because I'll not have my aunt troubled for a name I can't trust."

You knock anyway, politely, on the plain door, and before you've finished the third rap a thin, cracked voice calls from within. "Tamsin. Who is it? Let her in. She's got a step like a girl with a lute."

Tamsin stares at the door and then at you. "She can't see a hand in front of her face these ten years," she mutters, and holds it open.

Inside is one low room, warm and dim, with a rush-light on a shelf and a little hearth. In a chair by the fire sits a tiny old woman wrapped in a patched shawl, her eyes clouded white. Her hands move on her lap as though she were spinning thread. A cat on the hearth opens one eye and shuts it again.

She tips her head toward you. "Come where I can feel you," she says. "Say a name for me, child. Not hers. Yours."

DM: You kneel by the chair, close enough that her hands can find you. Her fingers are light and dry as paper. They go over your face the way a weaver feels cloth, across the brow, down the cheek, and last to the chin. She stops there for a long moment.

"Wren," she says. It isn't a question. "Aye. She called you that, the one who brought you. I called you Mouse, because you wouldn't speak." Her clouded eyes fill, and she doesn't wipe them. "Tamsin, put the kettle on. And bar the door."

Tamsin does both, and she is gentler than she looked on the step.

Then Annis tells it, slowly. Elowen came on a cold autumn evening, fevered and thin, with a small girl asleep on her shoulder. She paid a silver coin and a promise. "I was to keep you, and ask nothing, and give you a toy, a little tin lantern, to whoever came saying her own name and not asking for another." She's quiet a while. "She came back once, at midwinter, and held you all night. Then she went away down the river road, and I never saw her face again."

In the spring a silver-haired man came with a better offer and a warm voice. "I was poor, child, and afraid, and he talked like a priest. I let him take you. I've been sorry for it every year since."

There is more. The year after, a different man came to the door, and Annis's voice drops. "Courteous. Quiet. A grey cloak, and a ring on his hand with a green stone in it that held the light. He asked after a girl, a small one, with her mother's chin. I told him you'd died of the fever and were buried in the reeds. He looked at me a long time and thanked me kindly. And then he paid for my fire-wood all that winter, which frightened me more than a blow would have."

She reaches under the shawl and draws out a little tin lantern, no bigger than a hen's egg, painted with a crooked flame and worn nearly smooth by old thumbs. It opens on a hinge. Inside is a slip of paper in your mother's hand: *She is Wren. Whoever asks for her by another name is no friend of hers.* You put it in your hand, and it fits.

"I kept it fifteen years," Annis says. "It was always yours."

You now hold the tin lantern toy, and you have a new mark for your enemy: a green-stone ring on a courteous hand. It is nearly six in the evening. Outside, the light is going orange over the river, and Kit waits with the horses. Tamsin is staring at the shutters and says quietly, "There was a rider on the dike road at noon who didn't come down. I'd thought nothing of it."

What does Wren do?

DM: You step out into the yard with the rapier low but ready. Kit ties his wrists with a length of ferry-rope, and Tamsin binds the torn arm with a strip of her own apron, none too gently. You lay out your terms. He looks from the hook in Tamsin's fist to Kit's crossbow, then back to you.

Your Intimidation check is a natural 20, and he comes apart all at once.

"Dace," he says. "Dace Rook. I'm hired, miss, not sworn, I swear it on my mother's grave." Yes, there was a ring. A leaf-green stone in a plain band, on the master's left hand, and he turns it when he's thinking. Dace has only seen him twice, across a lamp-lit table, and never his face fully. The master calls himself the natural son of the old Aldous Verrel, "born on the wrong side of the blanket and robbed of Thornhallow." Dace says it quickly, like a story he has heard told too many times. "He says there's a leaf cut out of the chapel's register that proves it. He wears his dead father's name till he can prove it's his." He swallows. "That's all I know of that, I swear."

And then, with the same fear: his orders were to follow you from Saltmarsh and signal from the dike with a shuttered lantern, so Sparrow, who waits in a skiff at Fenny Reach, a drowned boat-yard two miles downriver, would know where you slept. If no signal came by dawn, Sparrow would come looking. "The master's orders for you," he says, very low, "are to take you and anything of your mother's alive, and leave nobody to talk after."

Behind you, Tamsin spits into the dust. Inside the house, Annis's cracked voice calls again, quiet and steady: "Tamsin. Is it done?"

It is nearly one in the morning. Dawn is about five hours off, you've 11 of 17 hit points, one 1st-level slot, and two Bardic Inspirations. You have a prisoner who is also the signal Sparrow is waiting for. What does Wren do?

He agrees to a silver and a promise of breakfast, with a grunt that is nearly a smile. You pay the silver, so you have 70 gp 6 sp 1 cp.

The crossing is cold and still. Halfway out, the guard boat's lantern swings toward you out of the dark. You hail it as Dunn brings the skiff alongside, and a broad, laconic man with a clove in his teeth leans over the rail. "Hesk Dolan," he says. "The arbiter said a girl with a lamp-staff might pass. Quiet night, mostly." He shifts the clove. "Around midnight a grey sail, no lights, crossed the estuary mouth, turned, and went north toward Sedge Creek. We kept our lantern up, and she didn't like it." He nods at the light on the spit. "Your old man's still up there. He waved at us twice."

The sky is going pearl-grey when the skiff grounds at the weedy landing. The Gullspit light burns white above you, steady, with no scorch on the stone and no mark on the door. It is a quarter to six. Brann has the door open before you have knocked. He is thinner than three days ago and wild with cold and joy, but unhurt. He hugs you without asking, then lets go, embarrassed, and ushers you in.

In the lantern-room, the great lamp turns slowly inside its ring, the reliquary's foot seated at its base, and the whole room hums with a clean, quiet light.

Over a pot of hot tea, Brann tells you what he didn't say before. "There's a door in the cellar. Iron. Sealed sixteen years, with a keyhole shaped like a lantern. Elowen told me never to open it without the foot, and I've never tried." He turns his cup in his hands. "The foot's in the lamp up there. Take it to open the door, and the light goes dark."

You've three 1st-level slots, all your Inspiration, and the morning ahead. The foot is the key to something, and the lamp is the lock the master said. Taking the foot darkens the light, and that costs you the beacon Saltmarsh has been watching for three days. Leaving it keeps the light and keeps the door shut. The guard boat will stay near until noon. What does Wren do?

  [game] notebook: updated The Gullspit Light
DM: Brann leads the way down a stair slick with old salt and holds the candle high. The cellar is low and round, floored with flagstones and ringed with rust-brown scars where seawater has stood. The iron door is set into the far wall, black and square, with no handle.

You take your time with it, with Kit at your shoulder and Brann's candle shaking the shadows. Your Investigation check is a 17, and the door gives up its secrets.

There are no needles, no runes, and no hidden catches. At the centre is a lantern-shaped plate with a socket in it, and your reliquary's foot would fit it exactly. Scratched into the iron beside it in your mother's tilted hand are two small marks: a single arrow turning left, with the words *once, for the road*, and two arrows turning right, with the words *twice, for the sea*. Whatever that means, it reads like a warning or a choice.

You also see what the cellar has been telling you. A green line of weed runs a hand's breadth above the threshold, so this chamber floods at the highest tides, and the door's lower hinge is rust-pitted but sound. The tide is climbing toward its morning high at about nine. You have three hours before the water reaches the stairs.

So the door isn't a trap, but it isn't simple either. The foot turns left once for the road, or right twice for the sea. You don't know which one opens it, and you don't know what the other does.

You have three 1st-level slots, all your Inspiration, Kit at your shoulder, Brann holding the candle, and Dunn waiting at the landing. The lamp is still burning upstairs. What does Wren do?

DM: You say the words and draw the sign in the air, and the ritual takes ten quiet minutes, long enough that Brann's candle gutters twice and Kit shifts her weight. Then the spell opens like a second sense behind your eyes.

You sense magic in two places. The lantern plate on the iron door shows a thin, steady Abjuration aura, the kind that guards. Around the door, set into the flagstones in a ring like a faint chalk line, is a second, fainter aura. It reads as Conjuration, and you have the odd idea that it is waiting. The iron door is thick enough to block the spell, so you learn nothing about what lies beyond it. You don't learn which turn is which, because the spell does not say, but the two auras fit the two warnings: one turn for a guard, one turn for the sea.

You keep the spell running and climb the stair, and Kit watches the dark behind you with her crossbow ready. In the lantern-room the great lamp turns in its ring, and the white light seems to lean toward you. You lift the reliquary's foot out of its socket and the light dies, instantly and completely. The whole tower goes cold and grey, and Brann says nothing but breathes out hard behind you. Through the cracked glass the guard boat's lantern is a small yellow star on the water, and Dunn can be seen on the landing.

The reliquary is back in your hand and in your pack. By now it is ten past six, with the tide still rising. You go back down the stair with Kit, and Detect Magic is still on you with a few minutes left. The lantern plate waits in the cellar, and so does the choice. Do you turn the foot once to the left for the road, or twice to the right for the sea? Or do you try something else first? What does Wren do?

DM: "Everyone up two steps, in case I'm wrong," you say. Kit takes Brann by the elbow and they climb, and Kit's crossbow stays on the stair. You seat the reliquary's foot in the lantern plate, it fits with a click, and you turn it once to the left, for the road.

Deep in the iron something shifts, slow and heavy, like a key in a very old lock. The ring of Conjuration in the flagstones stays quiet and dark. The door breathes out a smell of cold stone and dry paper, swings inward on a hinge that makes no sound, and does not flood the cellar.

Beyond it is a small stone cell, dry as a bone, no bigger than a cupboard. On a shelf cut into the wall is a lead-lined tube, a little brass lantern the size of a thumb, and a folded letter. The tube has a faint ward on it that your Detect Magic, still running, reads as Abjuration. Nothing else is in the room, and nothing moves.

The letter is in your mother's tilted hand:

*My wren, if the door opened to the road, then you are who I hoped, and you are kind enough not to have drowned us both. In the tube is a leaf I cut from the Thornhallow register, which an old chaplain gave me because he knew a man would one day come to burn it or to use it. One side is a baptism, Aldous Verrel the younger, a servant's child, his father consenting. The other side is the child's burial, three years on. The boy is dead, and he has been dead thirty years. A man who comes to you wearing that name wears a dead child's grave. He is not what he says. He first came to me as a patron of our shrines, and then asked for the lamp. Do not let him make you his. Burn the leaf if you must, keep it if you can, and whatever you choose, choose it as yourself. Light the road, Wren. —E.*

You read the leaf by Brann's candle: the baptism on one side, and the burial on the other, in the same cramped chaplain's hand. It matches the register copy you carry, and the note about the missing page in Brother Osric's book. It proves the Grey Cloak's story false. It also proves nothing about who he really is.

You take the leaf, the letter, and the little brass lantern (Elowen's last gift is a thumb-sized lamp with a wick the size of a whisker). You take the foot back out of the plate and shut the iron door. Ten minutes, then twenty, pass in the cellar as you read and think. It is nearly half past six in the morning, and the lamp upstairs is dark. The tide is still rising. The guard boat waits on the water, and Dunn is yawning on the landing.

You've got the proof. You've got your mother's words. The reliquary's foot goes back in the lamp if you want the light burning, or stays with you if you want to carry the key. And the Grey Cloak will come for the leaf, if he's learned it's yours. What does Wren do?
